L487: ## Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L488: In Tanaka et al. 2008, a total of 12 patients were enrolled, 4 into the placebo arm, 5 into the low dose group, and 3 into the high dose group. None of the 4 placebo-treated patients achieved a HR decrease of \ge20% with a HR &lt;100 bpm, whereas 4 (80%) patients in the low dose group and 2 (67%) in the high dose group achieved the HR reduction. Overall, thus the number achieving the post-hoc endpoint was 6 (75%) of the 8 landiolol-treated patients. By 11 minutes of administration, the mean HR increased in the placebo group by 2.3%, whereas in the low dose group the mean HR decreased by 26% and the high dose group by 22%.

L493: ## 6.5. Taenaka and Kikawa 2013
L494: ## 6.5.1. Study Design
L495: ## Overview and Objective
L496: Citation: Taenaka N, Kikawa S. The effectiveness and safety of landiolol hydrochloride, an ultra-short acting b1-blocker, in post-operative patients with supraventricular tachyarrhythmias: a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study. Am J Cardiovasc Drugs. 2013a Oct;13(5):353-64.
L497: A randomized placebo-controlled double-blind multi-center parallel group clinical trial of landiolol hydrochloride, in patients with ischemia and post-surgical tachycardia, to assess safety and effectiveness of landiolol for control of heart rate (HR) over 11 or 22 minutes of administration.
L498: ## Trial Design
L499: Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 is a randomized placebo-controlled double-blind multi-center parallel clinical superiority trial. Comparison with contemporaneous placebo is appropriate for the assessment of landiolol safety and effectiveness.
L500: Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 was performed at 38 centers in Japan from 2001 through 2002. Although completed entirely outside of the US, similar to Kato et al. 1997, Yoshiya et al. 1997, and Yoshiya et al. 2002, the study is felt to be applicable to the US population as the conditions paroxysmal AF and AFL and SVT are common in the US population and potential undocumented differences in the populations are believed to be unlikely to affect the mechanism of action nor the safety or effectiveness of the study drug.
L501: Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 enrolled consented patients ≥20 years old prior to scheduled surgery if they had either pre-existing ischemic heart disease defined by prior myocardial infarction or angina requiring treatment, ischemic electrocardiographic or echocardiographic changes, or hypertension requiring antihypertensive treatment. Alternatively, without known cardiovascular disease they could be enrolled if they were undergoing cardiovascular surgery,
L502: ## Landiolol hydrochloride
L503: or resection of esophageal cancer, a thoracotomy, or upper abdominal surgery. Postoperatively, to proceed in the study, patients needed a post-operative supraventricular tachycardia with HR \ge 120 bpm sustained for \ge 3 minutes for patients with sinus tachycardia, or HR \ge 120 bpm sustained for \ge 1 minute for patients with SVT or tachycardia that was not sinus rhythm. In all cases tachycardia was sustained after correction of known precipitating causes such as hypovolemia and anesthesia.
L504: Patients were excluded if they had a myocardial infarction within 1 month, 'severe' heart failure with NYHA III or IV, known second or third-degree atrioventricular heart block, or sick sinus syndrome. Patients were also excluded if they had used a beta-adrenergic receptor antagonist within 1 week prior to surgery. Patients who were pregnant or lactating, and those that had liver, kidney, or blood disorders defined by AST \ge 2.5-fold normal or 100 IU/L, ALT \ge 2.5-fold normal or 100 IU/L, blood urea nitrogen \ge 25 mg/dL, creatinine \ge 2 mg/dL, or total bilirubin \ge 3.0 mg/dL within 2 weeks prior to surgery, were also excluded. *There was no stated exclusion for low blood pressure reported for the trial.*
L505: Patients were prohibited from receiving concomitant antiarrhythmic medications including other beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists, calcium channel antagonists, or sympathomimetics, or cholinesterase inhibitors. If patients had been taking these medications, they had to have been discontinued for at least twice the half-life of the medication. However, if dopamine or dobutamine had been administered by continuous infusion at a dose &lt; 10 mcg/kg/min before administration of the study drug, concomitant administration at the same dose was permitted. Digitalis medicines used before administration of the study drug were also permitted if the pharmacological effect was confirmed to have reached a steady state and the bradycardic effect on the heart rate was constant. These medication exclusions are reasonable from a clinical standpoint.
L506: Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 used weight-based intravenous landiolol hydrochloride dosing, with random allocation to either a low dose or a high dose group, or placebo. In the low dose group, a 30 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 10 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then another bolus of 60 mcg/kg/min over 1 minute was given followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 additional minutes. In the high dose group, a 60 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then a 125 mcg/kg/min bolus dose was given over 1 minute followed by an additional 10 minutes of 40 mcg/kg/min. The highest dose was the same loading and continuous infusion dose used in Kato et al. 1997 whereas the initial lower dose regimen was used in Yoshiya et al. 2002. Landiolol was reconstituted in normal saline, which was used as the placebo arm infusion.
L507: Patients were continuously monitored during administration of the study drug, but no schedule of assessments was included in the publication.
L508: Dose discontinuation could occur if the targeted HR reduction (\ge 20 % reduction from the
L509: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
L510: ## Landiolol hydrochloride
L511: baseline HR and a HR of &lt;100 bpm) was obtained after administration of the initial dose. If the targeted heart-rate reduction was not obtained, a second 1-minute infusion and 10-minute continuous intravenous infusions were given at an increased dose. If the targeted heart rate reduction was obtained during the period of increased-dose administration, administration was completed. If hypotension defined by a systolic BP decrease of \ge 20\% from the baseline SBP and blood pressure \le 90/60 mmHg or marked bradycardia defined by a heart rate of \le 60 bpm occurred, administration was discontinued.
L512: Although stated to be “double-blind” neither the techniques used to ensure blinding nor randomization are described in the Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication. Although earlier trials have reported that maintaining blinding with a colorless solution diluted in normal saline, the methods to maintain such blinding cannot be presumed to have been employed.
L513: ## Study Endpoints
L514: As stated previously, for the purpose of regulatory approval, each submitted clinical trial was analyzed with respect to the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge 20\%, or achieved HR \le 100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently. HR is a surrogate outcome.
L515: The stated primary endpoint in the Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication was the proportion of patients who achieved an improved HR, defined as a \ge 20\% reduction from the baseline HR and a HR of &lt;100 bpm. In this setting, the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint closely matches the primary endpoint of the published clinical trial.
L516: Secondary endpoints included the change in HR, BP, and RPP, as well as ECG parameters.
L517: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L518: Because the published literature was used exclusively to support effectiveness and safety of landiolol for this application, individual patient-level data was unavailable. Thus, no formal statistical reanalysis on the patient data could be performed. Intention to treat or a treatment policy estimand is reported for the trial results.
L519: The Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication reports that the primary endpoint was evaluated by the Chi-square test for the proportion of patients who experienced improvement in HR between the treatment groups. The significance level was set at an alpha of 0.025 (two-tailed). Secondary endpoints were analyzed by Dunnett's multiple comparison and the Chi-square test. Significance was defined as P \le 0.05 (two-tailed).

L546: ## **Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)**
L547: No additional baseline information regarding the included patients was included in the publication.
L548: Treatment adherence, non-exclusion concomitant medications, and any explicit rescue medications used are not reported in the Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication.
L549: ## **Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint**
L550: In Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, a substantially greater proportion of patients achieved the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge 20\%, or an achieved HR \le 100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently with pooled landiolol treatment than with placebo, Table 33. Unfortunately, the primary endpoint is only reported for 146 of the 151 patients stated to be included in the full analysis set, leaving out 5 additional patients, 2 from the placebo group, 2 from the low-dose group, and 1 from the high dose group. If it is assumed for a sensitivity analysis that all the unreported patients in the placebo group met the primary endpoint, and none in the treated groups, then the pooled proportion of patients that met the primary endpoint would be 2 (4%) of the placebo group and 50 (50%) of the landiolol-treated group.
L551: As a reminder, in the low dose group, a 30 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 10 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then another bolus of 60 mcg/kg/min over 1 minute was given followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 additional minutes if patients had not achieved the primary endpoint of HR reduction. In the high dose group, a 60 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then a 125 mcg/kg/min bolus dose was given over 1 minute followed by an additional 10 minutes of 40 mcg/kg/min if patients had not achieved the primary endpoint of HR reduction
L552: The treatment effect for the 146 reported patients are broken down in Table 34. 60% of the patients in the low-dose group met the primary endpoint, 42% of patients in the high dose group met the primary endpoint, and no patients in the placebo group met the primary endpoint.
L553: The publication also states that following the first treatment doses in the low-dose and high-dose groups, the percentage of patients that met the primary endpoint were 8% for the low-dose (30 mcg/kg/min over 1 minute then 10 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes) and 22% for the high dose group (60 mcg/kg/min over 1 minute then 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes). Overall, following the initial dose (at 11 minutes of administration), the percentage change in HR was an increase of 1.5\%\pm 6.8\% for placebo, a decrease of 15.0\%\pm 7.4\% for the low-dose group, and a decrease of 13.7\%\pm 10.6\% for the high-dose group. Following the second dose (at 22 minutes of administration), the percentage change in HR was a decrease of 2.4\%\pm 7.9\% for placebo, a decrease of 23.3\%\pm 8.0\% for the low-dose group, and a decrease of 18.9\%\pm 10.0\% for the high dose group.

L575: No additional FDA analyses were undertaken due to the lack of individual patient data, however additional data were presented as part of the publication, including the effect on blood pressure and the adverse event rates. These are presented in Safety Results, section 8.4.
L576: ## 6.6. Sakamoto et al. 2012
L577: ## 6.6.1. Study Design
L578: ## Overview and Objective
L579: Citation: Sakamoto A, Kitakaze M, Takamoto S, et al. Landiolol, an ultra-short-acting \beta_1-blocker, more effectively terminates atrial fibrillation than diltiazem after open heart surgery: prospective, multicenter, randomized, open-label study (JL-KNIGHT study). Circ J. 2012;76(5):1097-101.
L580: A randomized active-controlled open-label multi-center parallel clinical trial of intravenous landiolol hydrochloride, in patients with post-operative AF, to assess safety and effectiveness of landiolol for control of HR when compared to intravenous diltiazem.
L581: ## Trial Design
L582: Sakamoto et al. 2012, also known as the Japan Landiolol kick-off of novel investigation for gold standard heart study (JL-KNIGHT Study), is a randomized active-controlled open-label multi-center, parallel group trial. Compared to the clinical trials described previously in this review, this is an unblinded open-label randomized trial with active control arm utilizing intravenous diltiazem infusion for the management of tachycardia. Diltiazem, as described in Section 2.2, Analysis of Current Treatment Options, is a non-dihydropyridine calcium-channel blocker indicated in the US for the temporary control of rapid ventricular rate in AF or AFL, and for the rapid conversion of paroxysmal SVT to sinus rhythm. Diltiazem is given intravenously as an initial bolus dose of 0.25 mg/kg over 2 minutes then 0.35 mg/kg repeat bolus if needed, then an infusion at 5 mg/hour that can be increased up to 15 mg/h. Thus, comparison with contemporaneous diltiazem is appropriate for the assessment of landiolol safety and effectiveness.
L583: Sakamoto et al. 2012 was performed at 36 centers in Japan from 2008 through 2009. Although completed entirely outside of the US, similar to Kato et al. 1997, Yoshiya et al. 1997, Yoshiya et al. 2002, Tanaka et al. 2008, and Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, the study is felt to be applicable to the US population as paroxysmal post-operative AF is common in the US population and potential undocumented differences in the populations are believed to be unlikely to affect the mechanism of action nor the safety or effectiveness of the study drug.
L584: Sakamoto et al. 2012 enrolled consented patients between 20 and 85 years old who were undergoing elective cardiothoracic surgery, but prior to surgery. Patients were excluded if they had a myocardial infarction within 3 days prior to enrollment, prior supraventricular arrhythmia that had required treatment, 'severe' heart failure with NYHA III or IV symptoms or left ventricular ejection fraction &lt;35%, known second or third-degree atrioventricular heart block, sick sinus syndrome, or contraindications to beta-adrenergic receptor blockade or calcium
L585: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
channel blockade. Additional exclusions were known AF with secondary causes such as electrolyte imbalance, Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome, or hyperthyroidism. Patients with BP &lt;90/60 mmHg were excluded, as well as patients with perioperative antiarrhythmic medication use other than digoxin.
L586: Following enrollment, the patients were randomly assigned to the study drug as delivered by the 'envelope method' and continuously monitored on cardiac telemetry. Further details of the randomization are not adequately described. It is unclear whether details of the randomization process could have affected the clinical trial results.
L587: The study drug was given if postoperative AF or AFL occurred with a ventricular HR \ge 100 bpm for \ge5 minutes. The diagnostic criteria used for AF or AFL were not described nor whether there was central adjudication of the arrhythmia. Although the diagnostic criteria for AF and AFL are not documented in the submitted trial materials, these are clinically common and have typical non-ambiguous electrocardiographic findings that can be reasonably expected to have been made by the clinical teams treating these patients.
L588: Sakamoto et al. 2012 used weight-based landiolol hydrochloride dosing as a continuous intravenous infusion without bolus initiated at 0.5 mcg/kg/min that could be titrated to 40 mcg/kg/min with changes every 10 minutes as determined to be necessary to achieve a reduction in HR on ECG. The comparator, diltiazem, was administered intravenously as a bolus dose of 0.25 mg/kg over 2 minutes followed by an initial infusion rate of 3 mg/hour that could be titrated up to 15 mg/hour, reassessed at 1-hour intervals. Both landiolol and diltiazem infusions were titrated to achieve the goal HR of &lt;90 bpm for patients in AF. The duration of infusion for both study drugs following onset of AF was 24 hours.
L589: If hypotension, defined as a systolic BP &lt;90 mmHg, or bradycardia, defined as a HR&lt;50 bpm, occurred, the dose of study drug was decreased or discontinued until resolution of hypotension or bradycardia.
L590: ## Study Endpoints
L591: As stated previously, for the purpose of regulatory approval, each submitted clinical trial was analyzed with respect to the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge20%, or achieved HR \le100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently.
L592: In Sakamoto et al 2012, the stated primary endpoint was the proportion of patients that converted to sinus rhythm from AF after 8 hours of study drug treatment. The secondary endpoints were the proportion of patients that converted to sinus rhythm by 24 hours of treatment, and the proportion of patients who achieved a HR &lt;90 bpm. HR and BP data were collected every hour following initiation of study drug infusion. If AF or AFL was converted
L593: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
to sinus rhythm, the patient was monitored on telemetry for 3 days after the discontinuation of the drug to detect the recurrence of arrhythmia.
L594: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L595: Because the published literature was used exclusively to support effectiveness and safety of landiolol for this application, individual patient-level data was unavailable. Thus, no formal statistical reanalysis on the patient data could be performed. In the context of Sakamoto et al. 2012, there is no stated statistical analysis plan, except to say that “Differences of conversion rates, the percentage of patients achieving rate control, and adverse events were analyzed with Fisher’s exact test or the chi-square test as appropriate. In all analyses, P&lt;0.05 was considered statistically significant.”
L596: ## Protocol Amendments
L597: No protocol amendments to the published trial were reported.

L610: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L611: Of the 420 enrolled patients in Sakamoto et al. 2012, 85 patients, 44 randomized to landiolol and 41 randomized to diltiazem were excluded due to missing data or lack of adherence to the study protocol. The details of the missing data and nonadherence are not described further, nor whether these patients developed post-operative AF and should be evaluated as part of the effectiveness or safety analyses. Given that the total number of patients who were included and developed AF numbered 82, the excluded patients is a greater number, meaning that if the excluded patients had substantial incidence of AF and were treated, the trial results could not be considered valid or robust. In addition, although 82 patients were diagnosed with post-operative AF, endpoint data are only included for 71, leaving 11 patients that were excluded from analysis for unclear reasons that could have also been due to protocol violations.

L633: ## 6.7. Nagai et al. 2013
L634: ## 6.7.1. Study Design
L635: ## Overview and Objective
L636: Citation: Nagai R, Kinugawa K, Inoue H, et al. Urgent management of rapid heart rate in patients with atrial fibrillation/flutter and left ventricular dysfunction: comparison of the ultra-short acting \beta_1-selective blocker landiolol with digoxin (J-Land Study). Circ J. 2013;77(4):908-16.
L637: A randomized active-controlled single-blind multi-center parallel clinical trial of intravenous landiolol hydrochloride, in patients with AF or AFL and left ventricular dysfunction, to assess safety and effectiveness of landiolol for control of HR when compared to intravenous digoxin.
L638: ## Trial Design
L639: Nagai et al. 2013, also known as the J-Land Study, is a randomized active-controlled single-blind multi-center, parallel group trial. Compared to the clinical trials described previously in this review, this is distinct as a single-blind randomized trial with active control arm utilizing intravenous digoxin infusion for the management of tachycardia. Digoxin, as described in Section 2.2, Analysis of Current Treatment Options, is indicated in the US for the control of resting ventricular rate in adults with chronic atrial fibrillation. However, in clinical practice digoxin is used for the management of AF and AFL and is identified in national and international guidelines for this purpose, as also described in Section 2.2, Analysis of Current Treatment Options. Digoxin is administered intravenously as a loading dose of 8-12 mcg/kg in adults, given as 50% initially then 25% every 6-8 hours twice to achieve the total load, then digoxin is given once daily at 2.4-3.6 mcg/kg for maintenance. Therefore, comparison of landiolol with
L640: contemporaneous digoxin is appropriate for the assessment of landiolol safety and effectiveness.
L641: Nagai et al. 2013 was performed at 95 centers in Japan between 2011 and 2012. Although completed entirely outside of the US, the study is felt to be applicable to the US population as the conditions AF and AFL are common in the US population and potential undocumented differences in the populations are believed to be unlikely to affect the mechanism of action nor the safety or effectiveness of the study drug.
L642: Nagai et al. 2013 enrolled consented patients \ge 20 years old with heart failure and NYHA III or IV symptoms and a left ventricular ejection fraction of 25-50%, with HR \ge 120 bpm in AF or AFL.
L643: Patients were excluded if they were deemed to need electrical cardioversion, had 'serious' valvular stenosis, confirmed or suspected hyperthyroidism, an implanted pacemaker or defibrillator, required mechanical ventilation, and if they had systolic BP&lt;90 mmHg as the definition of cardiogenic shock. Patients were prohibited from receiving concomitant antiarrhythmic medications, calcium channel antagonists, a defibrillator or pacemaker therapies, catheter ablation, until 2 hours after starting treatment, but chronic oral digitalis formulations and beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists were allowed to be continued as prescribed for chronic heart failure treatment. These medication exclusions and inclusions are reasonable from a clinical standpoint.
L644: Nagai et al. 2013 used weight-based landiolol hydrochloride dosing without a bolus. Landiolol hydrochloride was administered intravenously by continuous infusion initiated at 1 mcg/kg/min and increased to a maximum dose of up to 10 mcg/kg/min based on the patient clinical response and HR. Landiolol was administered for at least 2 hours and up to 72 hours. Digoxin was administered at an initial dose of 0.25 mg and could be increased within 72 hours if clinically indicated. If patients were taking chronic digitalis formulations, the load could be reduced to 0.125 mg of intravenous digoxin. As the mean weight of patients in the trial was 60.5 kg, as depicted in Table 41, the dose of 0.25 mg once corresponds to 4.1 mcg/kg initial bolus. This mean initial bolus is appropriate as half the loading dose, but because subsequent completion of the digoxin loading dose was not administered, this dosing of digoxin should be considered incomplete and under-dosed. Therefore, the comparison to the HR slowing effect of digoxin in this study may be a comparison to placebo or no treatment, rather than an effective HR slowing agent. The study schema is depicted in Figure 8.
L645: After completion of the trial observations at 2 hours, landiolol could be transitioned to an oral beta-adrenergic receptor antagonist if deemed appropriate by the treating physician.
L646: Randomization in Nagai et al. 2013 was by permuted block method. Although the publication states the trial to be 'single-blind,' neither the definition of single-blind nor the techniques used to ensure blinding are described in the publication. Although the previously described trials have reported that maintaining blinding with a colorless solution diluted in normal saline, the methods to maintain such blinding cannot be presumed to have been employed.
L647: ## Study Endpoints
L648: As stated previously, for the purpose of regulatory approval, each submitted clinical trial was analyzed with respect to the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge 20\%, or achieved HR \le 100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently. HR is a surrogate outcome.
L649: The Nagai et al. 2013 publication reports a primary endpoint that was the percentage of patients who achieved HR reduction with both a HR &lt;110 bpm and a \ge 20\% decrease from baseline HR at 2 hours of study drug administration. The secondary endpoints were HR at 0.5, 1, and 2 hours after initiation of the study drug, conversion to sinus rhythm, and subjective patient symptoms at these times. Safety endpoints included the incidence of adverse events.
L650: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L651: Because the published literature was used exclusively to support effectiveness and safety of landiolol for this application, individual patient-level data was unavailable. Thus, no formal statistical reanalysis on the patient data could be performed. Intention to treat or a treatment policy estimand is reported for the trial results.
L652: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
L653: The Nagai et al. 2013 publication states: "The primary endpoint was compared between the 2 groups using a linear probability model with HR and LVEF measured immediately before starting the study drug as covariates. The changes in HR and BP after starting the study drugs were compared between the 2 groups using a linear mixed-effects model with adjustment for HR, BP, and left ventricular ejection fraction before starting the study drug. The following covariance structures were considered: unstructured, compound symmetrical, first-order autoregressive, and Toeplitz. The covariance structure that provided the best fit according to the Akaike information criterion was used in the analysis. Assessment times were treated as categorical factors. Student's t-test was used to compare outcomes between the 2 groups at each time, while the paired t-test was used to compare values between baseline and each time within each group. Bonferroni correction was used for multiple comparisons, except for the change in BP, which was assessed as a safety parameter. Subjective symptoms and objective findings (palpitations, chest pain, dizziness, dyspnea, and edema) were analyzed using the Wilcoxon rank sum test for comparisons between the 2 groups and the Wilcoxon signed rank sum test for comparisons within each group. Values of P&lt;0.05 were considered statistically significant (2-sided)."

L681: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L682: Treatment adherence, non-exclusion concomitant medications, and any explicit rescue medications used are not reported in the Nagai et al. 2013 publication.
L683: ## Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L684: In the published results of Nagai et al. 2013 a substantially greater proportion of patients achieved a HR reduction of \ge 20\% with a HR &lt;110 bpm at 2 hours after administration of landiolol compared to placebo, as depicted in Table 42. Although the primary endpoint in the clinical trial was less restrictive with a goal HR of \le 110 bpm rather than 100 bpm as included in the proposed post-hoc endpoint, the data regarding the primary endpoint of the trial can be considered to give an estimate of landiolol effectiveness of a magnitude that is likely less than would be seen with a less stringent HR component for the endpoint.
L685: Nevertheless, the endpoint is only reported for 82 of the patients in the landiolol arm and 98 patients in the placebo arm, leaving missing data from those who were administered the study drug for 11 patients (12%) in the landiolol arm, and missing data for 9 patients (8%) in the placebo arm. If all the unaccounted patients treated with digoxin achieved the primary endpoint, and none of the unaccounted patients in the landiolol arm achieved the primary endpoint, then the percentage of patients in the landiolol arm would be 43%, and the percentage of patients who achieved the primary endpoint in the digoxin arm would be 21%, which still supports the presence of a treatment effect on HR. If the same sensitivity analysis is performed on the randomized population prior to exclusion for not receiving the study drug, the percentage of patients who achieved the primary endpoint would be 40% (40 patients out of 99) for the landiolol arm, and 19% (22 patients out of 115) for the digoxin arm.
L686: The percentage of patients who achieved the primary endpoint of a decreased in HR \ge 20\% with a HR &lt;110 bpm at 2 hours after administration are depicted in Figure 9. Percentage of patients who achieved the primary endpoint by landiolol dose in Nagai et al 2013. The mean dose of landiolol that was required at 2 hours was 6.7 \pm 3.2 mcg/kg/min, but there was no evidence of dose dependency, and the effective dose for HR reduction ranged across the tested doses from 1 to 10 mcg/kg/min.

L692: The loss of multiple patients from the full analysis set, including 14 patients who did not receive study drug, and then 2 who received landiolol, weakens the overall integrity of the data. Despite this, the sensitivity analyses are still supportive of the presence of a treatment effect for landiolol on HR in this population. Otherwise, data quality and integrity could not be further assessed given that the only submission was the published clinical trial manuscript.

L694: ## Durability of Response
L695: The HR response for patients who received landiolol or digoxin is graphed in Figure 10. The treatment effect on HR appeared durable over the 2 hours of administration. The decrease in HR from baseline to 2 hours of administration was 27.0 \pm 13.3 bpm for landiolol and was 16.0 \pm 13.0 bpm for digoxin.

These data at 48 hours of administration are shown in Table 43. The decrease in HR appeared durable for landiolol and for digoxin over the 48 hours of extended administration.

L701: ## Persistence of Effect
L702: Persistence of treatment effect with landiolol was not evaluated or reported in Nagai et al. 2013.
L703: ## 6.8. Xiao et al, 2014
L704: ## 6.8.1. Study Design
L705: ## Overview and Objective
L706: Citation: Xiao J, He P, Zou Q, Zhao Y, Xue Z, Deng X, Li S, Guo Q, Tao G, Yang T, Lang Z, He J, Wang X. Landiolol in the treatment of the intraoperative supraventricular tachycardia: a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study. J Clin Anesth. 2015 Mar;27(2):120-8.
L707: A randomized placebo-controlled double-blind multi-center parallel group clinical trial of landiolol hydrochloride in patients with intraoperative supraventricular tachycardia, to assess safety and effectiveness of landiolol for control of heart rate over 5 minutes of administration.
L708: ## Trial Design
L709: Xiao et al., 2014 is a randomized placebo-controlled double-blind multi-center parallel clinical superiority trial. Comparison with contemporaneous placebo is appropriate for the assessment of landiolol safety and effectiveness.
L710: Xiao et al., 2014 was performed at 8 centers in China from 2010 through 2012. Although completed entirely outside of the US, similar to the other randomized controlled trials of landiolol, the study is felt to be applicable to the US population as the conditions paroxysmal AF and AFL and SVT are common in the US population and potential undocumented differences in the populations are believed to be unlikely to affect the mechanism of action nor the safety or effectiveness of the study drug.
L711: Xiao et al., 2014 consented and enrolled patients between 18 and 70 years old prior to scheduled surgery. Patients were identified for possible inclusion in the treatment phase of the study after 30 minutes from the start of the surgery if HR \ge100 bpm but \le150 bpm and lasted for &gt;1 minute if the patient had SVT or &gt;3 minutes if the patient was in sinus rhythm.
L712: Patients were excluded if they had known cause of tachycardia such as hypovolemia, or if they had myocardial infarction within 1 month, 'severe' heart failure with NYHA IV symptoms, known second or third-degree atrioventricular heart block, or sick sinus syndrome, asthma, had
L713: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
L714: liver, kidney, or blood disorders defined by AST &gt;1.5-fold normal, ALT &gt;1.5-fold normal, or total bilirubin &gt;1.5-fold normal. Patients who were pregnant or lactating were excluded. Other exclusions included body mass index &lt; 18 or &gt;30 kg/m2, cardiogenic shock, diabetic ketoacidosis or metabolic acidosis, pulmonary hypertension associated with right ventricular dysfunction, congestive heart failure, pheochromocytoma. *There was no other stated exclusion for low blood pressure reported for the trial.*
L715: Xiao et al., 2014 used weight-based intravenous landiolol hydrochloride dosing with a bolus of 125 mcg/kg over 1 minute followed by 40 mcg/kg/min for 5 to 11 minutes. If the reduction of HR exceeded 10% at 5 minutes, intravenous landiolol at 40 mcg/kg/min was continued for 6 additional minutes. The control group received an intravenous placebo. The rescue medication esmolol could then be used when the reduction of HR did not exceed 10%. Intravenous esmolol was infused with a bolus of 500 mcg/kg over 1 minute then 50 mcg/kg/min for 4 minutes. If the esmolol dose was insufficient for HR reduction, the dose could be increased up to 300 mcg/kg/min for the continuous infusion. HR and BP were recorded before drug injection and 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, 35, 40, 45, 50, 55, and 60 minutes after the injection. The ECG was recorded before drug injection and 5, 10, 15, 30, and 60 minutes after the injection.
L716: ## Study Endpoints
L717: As stated previously, for the purpose of regulatory approval, each submitted clinical trial was analyzed with respect to the proposed post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge 20\%, or achieved HR \le 100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently. HR is a surrogate outcome. However, although the proportion of patients who achieved HR reduction can be inferred from the proportion of patients who received the rescue medication esmolol, because the use of the rescue medication was not mandatory, the treatment benefit cannot be reliably interpreted from the published data.
L718: The stated primary endpoint in the Xiao et al., 2014 publication was the proportion of patients who received the rescue medication esmolol when the reduction of HR did not exceed 10% after intravenous landiolol for 5 minutes. The proportion who achieved the post-hoc primary endpoint cannot be determined based on reporting of the stated publication primary endpoint.
L719: Secondary end points included HR, BP, and ECG assessments, time to decrease in HR of at least 10%, and time to HR &lt; 100 bpm.
L720: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L721: Because the published literature was used exclusively to support effectiveness and safety of landiolol for this application, individual patient-level data was unavailable. Thus, no formal statistical reanalysis on the patient data could be performed. Intention to treat or a treatment policy estimand is reported for the trial results.

L749: ## Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
L750: Treatment adherence and non-exclusion concomitant medications besides the rescue medication esmolol are not discussed in the Xiao et al. 2014 manuscript.
L751: ## Efficacy Results - Primary Endpoint
L752: In Xiao et al. 2014, the proportion of patients receiving the rescue medication esmolol was 7.6% (9 of 118) in the landiolol group and 80.3% (98 of 122) in the placebo group. The publication reports the P &lt; 0.0001, 95% confidence intervals -81.2% to -64.2%. Mean HR dropped from 110.3 \pm 8.6 bpm at baseline in the landiolol group to 86.0 \pm 10.4 bpm at 5 minutes after the administration, whereas the placebo group only declined from 110.0 \pm 9.6 bpm to 105.1 \pm 13.9 bpm after 5 minutes. The trend in HR is shown in Figure 11. Change in Heart Rate in Xiao et al. 2014. The proportion of patients who achieved the post-hoc primary endpoint of either a decrease in HR \ge 20\%, or achieved HR \le 100 bpm, or that the paroxysm terminated intermittently cannot be determined from the reported data in Xiao et al. 2014 or inferred from the reported primary endpoint in the publication.
L753: **Fig. 2** The reduction of HR after the administration of loading and continuous landiolol or placebo. ****Significant differences vs placebo (P &lt; .0001); *Significant differences vs placebo (P &lt; .05).
L754: ## Data Quality and Integrity - Reviewers' Assessment
L755: Data quality and integrity could not be assessed given that the only submission was the published clinical trial manuscript.
L756: ## Durability and Persistence of Response
L757: Change in HR was captured from 1 to 11 minutes of landiolol administration in Xiao et al. 2014 as depicted in Figure 11. The HR effect of landiolol was persistent up to 11 minutes of administration. Persistence of landiolol effect on HR following landiolol discontinuation were not reported.
L758: ## Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
L759: No additional FDA analyses were undertaken due to the lack of individual patient data, however additional data were presented as part of the publication, including the effect on blood pressure and the adverse event rates. These are presented in Safety Results, section 8.4.
L760: ## 7. Integrated Review of Effectiveness
L761: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
L762: ## 7.1. **Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials**
L763: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L764: ## 7.1.1. Primary Endpoints
L765: Taken together, the 6 randomized placebo-controlled clinical trials and the 2 randomized active-controlled clinical trials support the effectiveness of landiolol to lower HR in the short-term for patients with SVT. Although there is no clear dose-response relationship between dosing and achievement of HR reduction, the tested clinically employed doses appear to be effective when utilized in a monitored setting.
L766: Despite the disparate primary endpoints utilized in each of the randomized controlled trials that support the effectiveness of landiolol for HR reduction, described in Table 3, each trial included as a primary or secondary endpoint the effect of landiolol on HR in the short-term setting. Taken in their totality, these data overall support the assertion that landiolol is able to reduce HR for patients with SVT including those patients with AF and AFL.
L767: Although the details of the blinding processes used remain inadequately described, it is not entirely clear whether details of the blinding process could have affected the trials' results on the physiologic measurement of HR. Thus, it seems likely that there is a true effect of landiolol from the available evidence in spite of the lack of documentation regarding blinding.
L768: Overall, the included trial populations appear similar to patients who might receive landiolol treatment in the US, summarized in Table 47 and Table 48. A wide range of doses were studied in the submitted clinical trials, and although there was no evidence of dose responsiveness, the proposed label employs dosing within the studied range, also summarized in Table 47 and Table 48.

L783: ## 7.1.2. Secondary and Other Endpoints
L784: While tightly linked to the responder analysis primary endpoint of proportion of patients who achieved an at least 20% HR reduction with treatment, the presentation of the continuous HR response data is an alternative endpoint that is supportive of the treatment effect of landiolol on HR. These data are described for each clinical trial by Figure 2. Durability and persistence of heart rate response to landiolol in Kato et al. 1997, Figure 4. Durability and persistence of heart rate response to landiolol in Yoshiya et al. 1997, Figure 6. Durability and persistence of heart rate response in Yoshiya et al. 2002, Figure 7. Durability and persistence of heart rate response in Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, and Figure 10. Heart rate response to landiolol and digoxin in Nagai et al. 2013.
L785: ## 7.1.3. Subpopulations
L786: Due to the lack of individual-patient level data, subgroup analyses of the clinical trial data are not possible, and no relevant subpopulations have been identified. In addition, meta-analyses and complete pooling of the patient data are not possible due to the heterogeneity of the clinical trial populations and intervention dosing. Nevertheless, the consistency across these heterogeneous clinical trial populations supports a lack of treatment effect heterogeneity for landiolol among the populations enrolled in these clinical trials. A tabular summary of some of the common critical elements of the baseline populations are displayed in Table 51.

L791: ## Clinical Review
## Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
## NDA 217202
## Landiolol hydrochloride
L792: Trial	Comparator	Age (years)	Sex (% male)	Weight (kg)	SBP (mmHg)	HR (bpm)	% Atrial Fibrillation
Xiao 2014 (n=240)	Placebo	44.2 ± 11.5	30%	59.2 ± 9.4	120 ± 18	109 ± 8	Unknown
L793: ## 7.1.4. Dose and Dose-Response
L794: The applicant states: "Given the difference in dosing regimens (in dose and duration and fixed versus titration dose approaches), it is difficult to describe a specific dose-response relationship other than as a range." In addition, the "overlapping confidence intervals across the 5 placebo-controlled studies do not suggest that within the dose range and dosing regimens evaluated that there is a clear landiolol dose-related effect based on the target primary endpoint." "Data from uncontrolled studies with more than 1 dose suggest a relatively flat dose response curve and are difficult to interpret without a placebo control and the fixed dose approach."
L795: On independent review, despite that systemic exposure increases in a dose proportional manner at doses of 10 mcg/kg/min to 80 mcg/kg/min, there is not consistent evidence for a clear dose-response relationship for landiolol and its effect on HR. In the Yoshiya et al. (2000) publication, the proportion of surgical patients with tachycardia and HR \ge100 bpm during anesthesia who demonstrated moderate improvement in HR, defined by either intermittent termination of the paroxysm or HR reduction \ge20%, was 67% with landiolol at 10 mcg/kg/min, 78% at 20 mcg/kg/min, and 91% at 40 mcg/kg/min, which was statistically significant. However, in the Kato et al. (1997) publication, the proportion of patients with atrial fibrillation or atrial flutter and moderate improvement in HR was 56% with landiolol at 20 mcg/kg/min, 62% for 40 mcg/kg/min, and 69% for 80 mcg/kg/min, which was not statistically significant. Of note, the Kato et al. (1997) population was comprised of patients with atrial fibrillation and flutter with mean baseline HR 109-132 bpm and a separate population of patients with paroxysmal SVT and mean baseline HR 164-172 bpm, who were overall less responsive to landiolol treatment and demonstrated no dose response relationship to landiolol. Furthermore, Figure 9. Percentage of patients who achieved the primary endpoint by landiolol dose in Nagai et al 2013, demonstrates no evidence of dose dependency of effect of landiolol, as landiolol was effective for some patients at each of the tested doses from 1 to 10 mcg/kg/min. Therefore, there is no evidence to support dose responsiveness for effectiveness on a population level, and therefore the applicant decision to label only the lower range of tested doses, specifically 10 to 40 mcg/kg/min for patients without heart failure, and 1 to (b)/(4) mcg/kg/min for patients with heart failure, is concordant with the available data.
L796: ## 7.1.5. Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
L797: Across the submitted randomized controlled clinical trials, there was consistent rapid onset of action for landiolol with achievement of a reduction in HR by at least 20% in a substantial proportion of the treated population by 5-10 minutes after treatment initiation, as well as a consistent resolution of treatment effect on HR by 30 minutes after landiolol discontinuation. See Figure 2, Figure 4, and Figure 6 for detailed time-dependent graphical representation of the mean response on HR to landiolol administration in the setting of SVT. Over the time courses for which there is detailed follow up data regarding HR after landiolol discontinuation, there does not appear to be a substantial rebound increase in HR effect, but landiolol only appears to be effective for HR reduction while it is being administered.
L798: ## 7.2. Additional Efficacy Considerations
L799: ## 7.2.1. Considerations on Benefit in the Postmarket Setting
L800: Effectiveness of landiolol was primarily demonstrated in foreign clinical trials performed outside the United States (US). However, based on the generally accepted effect of beta-1 adrenergic antagonism it is reasonable to presume similar responsiveness to landiolol for patients in the US as patients outside of the US. Although some the clinical trials cited to demonstrate effectiveness of landiolol in the setting of SVT were performed 30 years prior to submission of this application, as reviewed in section 2.2 Analysis of Current Treatment Options, there have not been substantial changes in therapeutic options for these patients since these trials were performed. Finally, while the drug was frequently administered with a bolus dose followed by the infusion, based on the short half-life and onset of action of the drug, use of such a bolus is highly unlikely to have a clinically relevant effect compared to landiolol's use without a bolus on time to HR control.
L801: ## 7.2.2. Other Relevant Benefits
L802: Landiolol appears comparable to esmolol for the short-term reduction in HR for patients with SVT with regards to dosing scheme, rapidity of treatment effect onset, and persistence after discontinuation. There is no data with which to judge relative safety or effectiveness between landiolol and esmolol and therefore these medications cannot be judged to be distinguishable based on the available information. However, at the time of evaluation, esmolol has regulatory approval in the US, as described in Table 1.
L803: ## 7.3. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L804: Based on the sum of the evidence from multiple randomized controlled clinical trials, including 6 placebo-controlled trials, landiolol appears to be effective for reducing HR for patients with SVT including AF and AFL. Although the sponsor-proposed primary effectiveness endpoint is post-hoc, this post-hoc primary endpoint was often a component of the primary endpoint from each of the original published clinical trials, and the effect size appears large. Although the primary endpoint of HR reduction is a surrogate endpoint, this surrogate endpoint was previously agreed upon as an approvable endpoint for a similar agent that received regulatory approval following an advisory committee meeting. Although the clinical trials were performed exclusively outside of the US, principally in Japan, the data are clinically applicable to patients in the US, and it is expected that patients in the US will respond similarly to this specific beta-adrenergic receptor antagonist as they do to other beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists that have supportive data in the US population.
L805: - A statistically and clinically significantly greater proportion of patients with SVT and tachycardia treated with landiolol had decreased HR compared to placebo, according to the proposed post-hoc primary composite endpoint of either a \ge 20\%
L806: decrease in HR from baseline, decrease in HR to &lt;100 bpm, or conversion to sinus rhythm with treatment. The proportion of patients in each trial with a treatment response and therefore the treatment effects were large and their assessment does not rely on complicated statistical methodologies nor formal meta-analyses (Kato et al. 1997., Yoshiya et al. 1997., Yoshiya et al. 2002., Tanaka et al. 2008., Taenaka and Kikawa 2013., and from the active-controlled trial Nagai et al. 2013). In addition, the Xiao et al, 2014 publication supported effectiveness of a 10% HR reduction after 5 minutes of administration.
L807: - Compared to placebo, between 42% and 89% of patients treated with landiolol achieved the specified post-hoc primary endpoint of HR reduction, whereas only 0-11% of patients did if treated with placebo. For each of the submitted clinical trials, the post-hoc statistical hypothesis test supported a difference in treatment effect between landiolol and the placebo or active comparator. In addition, the hypothesis testing performed as part of the original clinical trials also appeared to support effectiveness of landiolol for HR reduction.
- Although the active controlled trials had substantial loss to follow up, compared to the active controls: 97% of patients treated with landiolol achieved the specified post-hoc primary endpoint of HR reduction, whereas only 78% did with diltiazem treatment; 43% of patients with heart failure treated with landiolol achieved the specified post-hoc primary endpoint of HR reduction, whereas only 12% did with digoxin treatment.
- Although the submitted clinical trial publications utilized heterogeneous populations with non-uniform inclusion and exclusion criteria, variable dosing and titration regimens, and the primary endpoint was post-hoc, the overall effectiveness of landiolol treatment was consistent among the submitted trials and populations.
- The treatment effect for landiolol was consistent for patients with tachycardia and a diagnosis of heart failure with a reduced left ventricular ejection fraction in the Nagai et al. 2013 publication.
- (b) (4)
- Although there is no evidence of biased publication, specifically clinical trials with landiolol that may have been performed but did not show evidence of a treatment effect on HR that may have been excluded from publication, and although such publication bias cannot be eliminated from possibility, the evidence of a treatment effect for landiolol in the enrolled populations is consistent among the multiple clinical trials performed by multiple investigators at multiple distinct institutions, all unconnected with the current applicant over multiple time periods. Thus, the potential for systematic publication bias that would substantially contradict the published evidence is considered unlikely.
L808: Therefore, the applicant has provided substantial evidence of effectiveness of landiolol for the short-term reduction of heart rate in the intended population of patients with SVT.
L809: The treatment effect, a reduction in HR for patients with SVT with a \ge 20\% decrease in HR from baseline, a decrease in HR to &lt;100 bpm, or conversion to sinus rhythm, is precisely the treatment effect for which esmolol was previously approved. It was believed by the Cardiovascular and Renal Drugs Advisory Committee meeting on September 29, 1986, that this treatment effect in the inpatient and monitored clinical setting is a clinically meaningful surrogate, as discussed in Section 5.2 Review Strategy. In that Cardiovascular and Renal Drugs Advisory Committee, in which the short-acting beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonist esmolol was discussed, it was believed that the hypothesis that perioperative HR-slowing would decrease the rate of clinical outcomes was untestable due to the universal application of this hypothesis in routine current clinical practice.27 This hypothesis has remained untested.
L810: There are no comparative effectiveness data comparing landiolol to esmolol for the short-term reduction in HR for patients with SVT with regards to dosing scheme, rapidity of treatment effect onset, and persistence after discontinuation. There are no data with which to judge relative safety or effectiveness between landiolol and esmolol and therefore these medications cannot be judged to be distinguishable based on the available information. While the Sakamoto et al. 2012 clinical trial compared landiolol to intravenous diltiazem for the management of HR, 13% of the data were missing even among the data elements that were reported in the Sakamoto et al. 2012 manuscript. Overall, this level of missing data makes the trial uninterpretable and noncontributory to the assessment of landiolol effectiveness or comparative effectiveness relative to the approved agent diltiazem. There are no other randomized controlled comparative effectiveness data for landiolol with respect to approved available therapies for HR control in the setting of SVT.
L811: ## 8. Review of Safety
L812: ## 8.1. Safety Review Approach
L813: The overall safety review of landiolol relies on the submitted but potentially incompletely ascertained adverse clinical events reported for each randomized controlled clinical trial of landiolol. These data are supplemented by the clinical and regulatory experience with multiple beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists for the purpose of HR control and management of other cardiovascular conditions, including metoprolol and propranolol reported in Table 1. Summary of Pharmacologic Treatments for Supraventricular Tachycardia (SVT). The utilized randomized controlled trials are listed in Table 2. Selected Clinical Trials Relevant to the Landiolol NDA. For the purpose of the safety review, an additional randomized controlled trial contributed data to the safety database that was not included in the primary effectiveness assessment, Xiao et al,
L814: 27 https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/drugsatfda_docs/nda/pre96/19-386_Brevibloc_AdminCorres.pdf
L815: To formulate estimates of adverse events (AE) with landiolol, safety data for each submitted clinical trial was individually interrogated for the purpose of the safety review. The relevant AEs associated with the drug landiolol were then pooled to create a reasonable estimate of the AE rates for patients treated with landiolol. The primary known safety concern with all of these medications, including those approved for the short-term management of HR in SVT, is hypotension, as described in Table 1. Summary of Pharmacologic Treatments for Supraventricular Tachycardia (SVT). Hypotension, though variably defined, was consistently described as an AE for some patients treated with landiolol and was a focus of the safety assessment for this application. Other AEs known to occur with the class of beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists, including bradycardia, bronchospasm, and heart failure were also specifically interrogated.
L816: The sponsor performed a broader interrogation of the uncontrolled non-randomized published literature that was also assessed for additional otherwise unidentified adverse events. The sponsor identified 41 total studies in which patients were administered landiolol with reported adverse event data, 25 of which were controlled, including the 8 randomized controlled studies included in Table 2. Selected Clinical Trials Relevant to the Landiolol NDA. Overall, 20 of the treatment and prevention trials were placebo-controlled, as depicted in Table 55. Published Placebo-Controlled Treatment and Prevention Population Adverse Events.
L817: ## 8.2. Review of the Safety Database
L818: ## 8.2.1. Overall Exposure
L819: The safety database of patients or healthy participants with reported adverse event data from the 8 randomized controlled trials of landiolol included in Table 2. Selected Clinical Trials Relevant to the Landiolol NDA, includes 568 patients with SVT treated with landiolol and 534 patients or participants treated with active control or placebo, described in more detail in Table 52. An additional 45 healthy volunteers received landiolol from the sponsor, and an additional 20 patients with SVT received landiolol from the sponsor in an uncontrolled trial. For the purposes of this review, the safety database is defined by treatment with the study drug, or the as-treated population. The safety database is partially limited by the lack of data reporting for some of the landiolol-treated patients overall, but there is safety data for the majority of patients. The 568 landiolol-treated patients enrolled in randomized controlled trials in the safety database appear to meet the minimum specified in the ICH E1A guideline, even though landiolol is not intended for long-term administration.28

L823: As the overall individual patient data cannot be pooled for this application, each clinical trial publication was interrogated and analyzed separately for safety. However, both the dosing and the patient populations were heterogeneous, which would have also limited the utility of pooling the data if it were possible. All dosing was short-term, most only for 5-22 minutes, with some controlled exposures up to 48 hours, as described in Table 2. Selected Clinical Trials Relevant to the Landiolol NDA. Therefore, the safety of landiolol during administration times greater than these cannot be determined from the currently available data. Of the 568 patients exposed to landiolol in studies for effectiveness for the treatment of SVT, only the 93 patients (16% of the total) in the Nagai et al. 2013 publication were treated for 48 hours, otherwise all patients only received landiolol for 5 to 22 minutes.

L831: ## 8.2.2. Relevant characteristics of the safety population:
L832: All patients studied in the randomized controlled trials of landiolol were located outside of the US, principally in Japan and China, Table 2. From what can be distinguished from the published clinical trials, the safety and effectiveness populations were similar or in some cases identical. As described in the demographics tables from each of the published clinical trials (Table 6. Demographic characteristics of the primary effectiveness analysis from Kato et al. 1997, Table 15. Demographic characteristics of the primary effectiveness analysis from Yoshiya et al. 1997, Table 22. Demographic characteristics from Yoshiya et al. 2002, Table 28. Demographic characteristics from Tanaka et al. 2008, Table 32. Demographic characteristics from Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, Table 37. Demographic characteristics from Sakamoto et al. 2012, Table 41. Demographic characteristics from Nagai et al. 2013, Table 45. Demographic Characteristics from Xiao et al. 2014), and in the summary demographics tables Table 47 and Table 48, as well as in Table 51, the patients given landiolol well-represented both sexes and patient age ranges that are typically affected by SVT including AF and AFL. Because AFL and SVT are common in the US population and in Japan and China, it is presumed that the treatment effect of landiolol would be similar between the non-US and US populations and not vary by racial or location differences. While the BMI of the treated populations likely does not fully encapsulate the current larger BMI range of the US population, it is expected that weight-based dosing with a rapid and monitorable treatment effect should allow safe administration of landiolol.
L833: Landiolol was tested in diverse clinical situations in the clinical trials submitted as evidence, Table 2. Selected Clinical Trials Relevant to the Landiolol NDA. The heterogeneity is a strength of the application, as these clinical situations include the varied clinical contexts in which landiolol is likely to be utilized by treating clinicians. These trials enrolled patients with both non-surgical and perioperative SVT, patients with cardiovascular risk factors, and patients with known cardiovascular disease including symptomatic heart failure. Patients with hepatic dysfunction were systematically excluded from the clinical trials of landiolol, and because there is potential for impact of hepatic dysfunction on the metabolism of landiolol and thereby the determination of appropriate dosing in patients with hepatic dysfunction, landiolol use cannot be deemed safe for those patients with significant hepatic dysfunction.
L834: ## 8.2.3. Adequacy of the safety database:
L835: Despite the absence of individual patient safety data that could be used for independent analysis, the total landiolol-exposed population was 568 patients in the 8 randomized clinical trials used for assessment of effectiveness and safety, Table 52. In addition, the absence of additional unexpected adverse events in the approximate (b) (4) patients included in the postmarketing data described in detail in Section 8.9 support the adequacy of the clinical trial safety database. The prevention clinical trial database of landiolol adds at least another 411 patients treated with landiolol at similar doses, summarized in Table 53. Description of Prevention-Only Placebo Controlled Studies of Landiolol and Table 54. Description of Prevention-Only Placebo Controlled Studies of Landiolol Continued, as well as in Table 55.
L836: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L837: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L838: No individual patient data were submitted as part of the applicant data package as all the clinical trial evidence was submitted from the published literature. Therefore, quality of data and adverse event ascertainment could not be rigorously assessed or reanalyzed for each clinical trial. In addition, full study protocols for each clinical trial were unavailable, including the methods of data collection or adverse event evaluation or adjudication. The lack of individual patient data does impair detailed assessment of effectiveness and safety in the context of heterogeneous treatment regimens and clinical trial populations, however these issues could not be otherwise addressed by the sponsor due to the inability to recover the primary data used to generate the publications submitted as evidence for effectiveness and safety. Therefore, the application was considered based on the inherent underlying but limited uncertainty present due to the lack of clinical trial protocol and individual patient data submission.
L839: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L840: Due to the unavailability of the clinical trial protocols and because the only available data come from the published literature, many of the details regarding AE collection in clinical trials of landiolol remain unknown. None of the landiolol clinical trials explicitly state how AEs were elicited or collected. In addition, no patient narratives were available for review, limiting independent assessment of AEs.
L841: The sponsor collected AE data from all the published placebo-controlled studies for which it was available and coded them according to the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms. These data do not include all patients who received landiolol, as AE data was not available for all patients, but the sponsor included only AEs for those 962 patients in published placebo-controlled trials for whom the AE data were published, tabulated in Table 55. Published Placebo-Controlled Treatment and Prevention Population Adverse Events.
L842: **Table 55. Published Placebo-Controlled Treatment and Prevention Population Adverse Events31**

L844: Most publications did not differentiate whether the reported AEs were felt to be related to the study drug, and when not reported it was assumed that the AE was related to the study drug. Due to the lack of individual patient data, but the reporting in the publications of the total number of AEs, the AE reporting assumes that each AE was attributed to a separate patient.
L845: *Reviewer Comment: Assuming that each AE is attributed to a single individual for each published trial is a conservative assessment, as typically AEs tend to cluster in fewer individuals, but is appropriate in this case as it aims to prevent underestimation of AE rates in the treated population.*
L846: Due to the short duration of landiolol administration in the majority of clinical trials, and the short duration of treatment effect after administration, most AE reporting only includes the time during which patients were administered landiolol and the brief period immediately afterwards, often only 30 minutes to 2 hours.
L847: The safety data are presented as system organ class (SOC) and by preferred term from MedDRA (version 22.1). The sponsor included AEs that occurred in &gt;2 landiolol-treated patients in tabulations of preferred terms. For the purposes of dose responsiveness regarding the AE rate, the sponsor grouped the analyses by dosing groups, with AE assigned to the lowest dose used in that patient population:

L850: ## 8.3.3. Routine Clinical Tests
L851: Laboratory testing in the clinical trials of landiolol suffered from the same liabilities as other data collection: typically, there was no published protocol or timeline of testing, testing was performed only in Japan and China using undescribed laboratory equipment and reference ranges, and in many cases no laboratory testing results are reported in the publications. Nevertheless, laboratory abnormalities were reported as AEs in some the landiolol clinical trials, and narrative descriptions of those treatment trials that included laboratory testing are described below. Of the reported laboratory abnormalities, although the magnitude of changes are not reported, there were no consistent patterns of abnormality and no consistent effect that could be attributable to landiolol treatment. Despite the minimal and inadequately described laboratory testing for patients treated with landiolol, the lack of clinically significant effect on laboratory values is consistent with the mechanism of action and the context of the intended short duration of treatment administration.
L852: In Kato et al. 1997, 1 patient in the landiolol group had a decrease in serum sodium and 1 patient in the placebo group had a decrease in serum sodium. There were no other laboratory abnormalities reported in the landiolol treatment group, described in Table 65.
L853: In Yoshiya et al. 1997, decreased leukocyte count occurred in 1 patient treated with landiolol and no patient treated with placebo. Increased AST and increased ALT occurred in 1 patient each treated with landiolol, and 2 patients treated with placebo had increased AST. One patient had increased total bilirubin in the landiolol-treated and in the placebo groups, 1 patient had a decrease in total cholesterol with landiolol, and 1 patient had a decrease in blood urea nitrogen in the landiolol group. There were no other laboratory abnormalities reported in the landiolol treatment group, described in Table 66.
L854: In Yoshiya et al. 2002, 7 patients in the landiolol group (26%) and 9 patients in the placebo group (33%) experienced a laboratory AE, Table 67. Numerically, for each laboratory abnormality, more patients experienced the change in the placebo arm than the landiolol arm, except for a decrease in platelet count, which occurred in 3 patients treated with landiolol and only 2 patients treated with placebo, and a decrease in total cholesterol, which occurred in 3 patients treated with landiolol and 2 patients treated with placebo.
L855: No laboratory AEs were reported in Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, Sakamoto et al. 2012, Nagai et al. 2013, or Xiao et al. 2014.
L856: ## 8.4. **Safety Results**
L857: ## 8.4.1. **Deaths**
L858: Only one patient was reported to have died in all of the published randomized controlled clinical treatment trials of landiolol. The patient who was reported to have died following treatment with landiolol in Nagai et al. 2013 was a patient who experienced an exacerbation of heart failure that developed 12 hours after completion of the 48-hour administration of landiolol; this same patient died 31 hours after completion of the administration of landiolol. There was also one death in the digoxin arm of Nagai et al. 2013, from sinus arrest. No other deaths were reported in any of the randomized controlled treatment trials. Given the overall short duration of administration, it is not surprising that no deaths occurred during the course of landiolol treatment in these clinical trials. Unfortunately, long-term follow up was not reported for most of the trials, so deaths occurring following short-term administration could not be reliably assessed. Additionally, because only 1 death occurred in the landiolol arms of the treatment clinical trials, the overall risk of landiolol associated mortality is assessed to be low.
L859: ## 8.4.2. **Serious Adverse Events (Hypotension)**
L860: AE rates and serious AE rates between landiolol-treated patients and those who received placebo in the entire safety population including both treatment and prevention trials were similar, with 157 events in the 962 landiolol-treated patients, and 126 in the 799 placebo-treated patients; with assumption of each event representing a distinct patient these data imply a rate of 16.3% for any AE in the landiolol treated population. The major preferred terms were hypotension, hypertension, tachycardia, atrial fibrillation, and abnormal laboratory testing, as delineated in Table 56 for only those adverse events for which there were &gt;2 patients affected. Due to the lack of individual-patient data, it could not be determined whether older patients, or those in other clinical or demographic subgroups were at higher risk of AE when treated with landiolol.

L865: The most common and serious of the adverse events identified associated with landiolol was hypotension, or a clinically significant decrease in BP, which in this accounting occurred in 6.1% of the landiolol-treated patients. In some of the randomized controlled trials hypotension was defined by a BP &lt;90/60 mmHg, in others hypotension was not defined. Hypotension is interpreted to be causally related to landiolol administration given the consistent higher occurrence in landiolol treated patients compared to placebo, the rapid BP reduction that temporally occurs with landiolol administration (Figure 12, Figure 13, Figure 14, Figure 15, Figure 16, Figure 17, Figure 18), the occurrence of hypotension in response to other beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists (Table 1), and the frequent resolution of BP changes with landiolol removal. However, there was no association between the rate of AEs overall or of hypotension with landiolol dose, with doses defined as described in Section 8.3.2, (LL): Maintenance dose of 1 to &lt;10 mcg/kg/min; (L): Maintenance dose of 10 mcg/kg/min; (M): Maintenance dose of 20 mcg/kg/min; (H): Maintenance dose of 40 mcg/kg/min; (HH): Maintenance dose of 80 mcg/kg/min. The dose relationship for landiolol and AEs for those patients in the treatment and prevention placebo-controlled clinical trials is described in Table 57. L885: Interrogation of the individual trial BP data demonstrated similar landiolol effects and AE rates. BP safety data for each submitted clinical trial is presented subsequently. Overall, these data demonstrate that the effect of landiolol on BP was consistent among the clinical trial populations and that the temporal change in BP was consistent with the known pharmacokinetics of landiolol (Section 4.5), with onset within 5 minutes of initiation of landiolol administration and resolution of BP effects within 30 minutes in the majority of patients.
L886: In Kato et al. 1997, the mean BP response was relatively mild with regards to change in systolic and diastolic BP over the course of treatment and following the end of administration, shown in Figure 12. A= landiolol and P=placebo. Whereas systolic BP dropped by mean 4 mmHg in the placebo arm after 11 minutes of treatment, it declined by 9 mmHg in the landiolol arm. The mean diastolic BP decline with landiolol at 11 minutes of administration was 4.3 mmHg and it was 2.3 mmHg with placebo.

L915: ## 8.4.3. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Effects
L916: Due to the short duration of administration of landiolol in the majority of the randomized controlled trials for treatment of SVT, typically ranging from 5 to 22 minutes, there were few discontinuations of treatment reported. In total, from the randomized controlled treatment trials for SVT, 13 patients treated with landiolol had administration discontinued due to AEs, of the total 568 patients treated with landiolol (2.3%). The majority of these events were aligned with the reported clinical trial protocols in that the administration of landiolol was discontinued
L917: Clinical Review
Mitchell Psotka, MD PhD
NDA 217202
Landiolol hydrochloride
L918: for a pre-specified AE, typically hypotension. Patients did not appear to drop out of the clinical trials due to these AE events or landiolol discontinuation and their data was able to be included in the safety and effectiveness analyses. Nonetheless, the patient disposition remains unclear for those treated in Sakamoto et al. 2012, as described in Section 6.6.2 and Table 36. Due to these issues, the Sakamoto et al. 2012 clinical trial was excluded from the safety and effectiveness analyses.
L919: Narrative reviews of the landiolol discontinuations are as follows:
L920: In Yoshiya et al. 1997, 2 patients out of 136 treated with landiolol, or 1.5%, discontinued treatment due to AEs. In both cases the AE event was hypotension that occurred at 5 minutes or 6 minutes of administration.
L921: In Yoshiya et al. 2002, 1 patient had landiolol discontinued after 1 minute of administration due to ECG change in ST-segment from -0.05 mV to -0.10 mV with a RPP of 16,909 bpm x mmHg, which was clinically determined to be an excessive hemodynamic workload, but no further data is reported regarding this patient. Another single patient had landiolol infusion discontinued due to hypotension, which reportedly resolved after subsequent discontinuation of the concomitant inhaled anesthetic. Landiolol was able to be continued in the other patients who developed hypotension in Yoshiya et al. 2002.
L922: In Taenaka and Kikawa 2013, 5 patients with AEs of hypotension with landiolol administration had landiolol discontinued leading to resolution of the hypotension.
L923: In the Nagai et al. 2013 trial, where patients would be treated up to 24 hours, there were AE-associated discontinuation events reported, and the only AEs that led to study drug discontinuation in the landiolol arm occurred within the first 2 hours of treatment, Table 70. The rate of landiolol discontinuation in this setting was 3% or 3 of 93 patients that received landiolol in the first 2 hours of administration. The 3 patients who had landiolol discontinued due to AE had 1 each of an embolic stroke, hypotension, and asthma exacerbation.
L924: In Xiao et al. 2014, patients were switched from landiolol to esmolol for ineffectiveness on HR reduction, but these landiolol discontinuations were not due to AE occurrence. See Section 6.8.2 for details. A single patient discontinuation due to AE was reported in the Xiao et al. 2014 clinical trial, but the etiology of the AE is not reported.

L946: ## 8.4.5. Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
L947: As described above in Section 8.4.2, the most common Treatment Emergent AEs (TEAEs) consisted of hypotension (Table 56. L948: The Kato et al. 1997 publication reports adverse events in 6% of enrolled individuals, with only one episode of decreased BP, as described in Table 65. All of the adverse events resolved or improved with or without treatment.

L961: The Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication reports a total of 74 adverse events, 28 adverse events in 11 (20%) of patients treated with placebo, and 46 that occurred in 25 (43%) of
L962: patients treated with landiolol. Only those adverse events judged to be related to the study drug are reported in additional detail in the Taenaka and Kikawa 2013 publication, which limits full interpretation of the adverse event profile of landiolol in the enrolled population. Nonetheless, all the adverse events judged to be related to the study drug were related to the cardiovascular system and the most frequent was hypotension defined by a systolic or diastolic blood pressure \le 90/60 mmHg.
L963: The description of each of the adverse event cases judged to be related to study drug are displayed in Table 68. LM= low dose group, in which a 30 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 10 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then another bolus of 60 mcg/kg/min over 1 minute was given followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 additional minutes if patients had not achieved the primary endpoint of HR reduction. MH= high dose group, in which a 60 mcg/kg/min bolus was given over 1 minute followed by 20 mcg/kg/min for 10 minutes, then a 125 mcg/kg/min bolus dose was given over 1 minute followed by an additional 10 minutes of 40 mcg/kg/min if patients had not achieved the primary endpoint of HR reduction. PP= placebo group.
L964: Each of the cases of hypotension that occurred with landiolol improved with discontinuation of landiolol administration, though some required administration of intravenous crystalloid or colloid solutions, Table 68. For once case procainamide was administered for unclear reasons.

L967: The Sakamoto et al. 2012 publication reported adverse events of hypotension and bradycardia with administration of landiolol, defined by BP &lt;90/60 mmHg and HR &lt;60 bpm, respectively. These adverse events are reported for the 71 patients without missing data in Table 69. Of the 35 patients with AF and without missing data treated with landiolol, 4 (11%) experienced hypotension, whereas of the 36 patients without missing data treated with diltiazem, 11 (31%) experienced hypotension. Nevertheless, due to the 13% missing data, or 11 patients enrolled who experienced AF and received one of the study drugs, the rate of adverse events in the landiolol group could have been substantially higher. If all 11 patients with missing data were in the landiolol arm and experienced hypotension, the percentage would be 33%. If only half of the missing patients were in the landiolol arm and all experienced hypotension, then the percentage with hypotension would be 24%. Thus, the Sakamoto et al. 2012 data can be interpreted as implying that the rate of hypotension defined by BP &lt;90/60 mmHg occurring with landiolol therapy in the post-operative management of AF is at least 11%.
