L971: The Nagai et al. 2013 publication reports that adverse events occurred in 30 (32%) patients in the landiolol treatment arm and 35 (33%) patients in the digoxin treatment arm. At the 2-hour time point, adverse events had occurred in 8 patients treated with landiolol and 2 patients treated with digoxin. Hypotension specifically was identified in 7 patients (7%) in the landiolol group and 4 patients (4%) in the digoxin group, and the landiolol infusion was stopped in 1 patient treated with landiolol due to hypotension. Worsening heart failure occurred in 1 patient treated with landiolol, and another treated with landiolol experienced an embolic stroke. Sinus arrest, diabetes insipidus, and pneumonia each occurred in 1 patient treated with digoxin. The overall incidence of all reported adverse events in Nagai et al. 2013 are included in Table 70.

L982: ## 8.4.6. Laboratory Findings
L983: Laboratory findings with landiolol therapy are discussed in Section 8.3.3 and reported in the tables in Section 8.4.5.
L984: ## 8.4.7. Vital Signs
L985: See Sections 8.4.2 and 8.4.5 for full details as to the BP response following landiolol administration. See Section 7, the Integrated Review of Effectiveness for full details as to the HR response following landiolol administration. As described for each clinical trial in sections and cumulatively for BP in section, landiolol caused a predictable and intended decrease in HR for patients with SVT, with a concomitant predictable decrease in systolic and diastolic BP that resulted in clinical hypotension, most often defined as a BP &lt;90/60 mmHg, in approximately 10% of treated patients (Table 60. Adverse Events and Hypotension in Placebo-Controlled Clinical Treatment Trials of Landiolol).
L986: ## 8.4.8. Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L987: No clinical trial publication reported which study personnel read the ECGs and whether there were particular criteria used for their interpretation. No primary ECG data were submitted for direct interrogation. While patients were presumably monitored with telemetry or continuous ECG during landiolol administration in the hospitalized setting, there is no description of the monitoring methodology, equipment, or sampling procedures used in the landiolol clinical
L988: trials. In total, at least one parameter was reported for 224 ECGs prior to landiolol administration and 221 ECGs after landiolol administration, as summarized in Table 72. Despite these limited data, landiolol appeared to have only expected effects on ECG parameters, with slowing ventricular rates, prolongation of the PR interval, and mild HR-dependent prolongation of the QT interval, given its mechanism of action. None of the changes in ECG parameters with landiolol treatment are considered to be clinically concerning.

L1006: Landiolol is a beta-1 adrenergic receptor antagonist and was tested in a thorough QT study without substantial QT altering effect. Landiolol metabolites 1 and 2 were also tested. Overall findings were consistent with low risk of Torsades de Pointes due to landiolol. The changes in ECG parameters including QT prolongation with landiolol in the published clinical trials also support the safety of landiolol with regards to the QT interval, as depicted in Table 73, Table 74, Table 75, and Table 76.
L1007: ## 8.4.10. **Immunogenicity**
L1008: There are not immunogenicity concerns with landiolol.
L1009: ## 8.5. **Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues**
L1010: ## 8.5.1. **Hypotension**
L1011: The most common and clinically relevant adverse event reported with landiolol administration was a reduction in blood pressure with episodes of hypotension, as described in detail in Section 8.4.2, Serious Adverse Events. Although variably defined, and with clinical significance unclear in some cases, hypotension is the expected adverse event for the class of beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists apart from their principal mechanism of action, which is to slow HR. Hypotension occurred at a rate of approximately 10% in landiolol-treated patients with SVT compared to 1.3% for patients with SVT treated with placebo. Although hypotension can be clinically significant, and hypotension long-after the expected resolution of pharmacodynamic effect of landiolol would end was associated with a mortality in the Nagai et al. 2013 clinical trial, given the short duration of action of intravenous landiolol, the predictable association with the effect of BP contemporaneously with change in HR, and the ability to monitor both vital sign changes in the inpatient setting where landiolol is intended to be used support that landiolol can be safety utilized for the treatment of SVT in this setting.
L1012: ## 8.6. **Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups**
L1013: Due to the absence of individual patient data, the occurrence of blood pressure reduction and hypotension could not be analyzed by demographic subgroups.
L1014: ## 8.7. **Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials**
L1015: No randomized clinical trials were performed as specific safety studies.
L1016: ## 8.8. **Additional Safety Explorations**
L1017: ## 8.8.1. **Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development**
L1018: Carcinogenicity was not assessed due to the short-term nature of the treatment.
L1019: ## 8.8.2. **Human Reproduction and Pregnancy**
L1020: No known pregnancies or reproductive outcomes, including landiolol use in patients who were lactating, occurred during in any of the published clinical trials using landiolol. There is insufficient clinical data to understand the risk of landiolol to cause developmental adverse outcomes. There is no data that exist regarding the presence of landiolol in human breast milk, its potential effect on a breastfed infant, or on breast milk production.
L1021: ## 8.8.3. **Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth**
L1022: None of the randomized clinical trials submitted as part of the evidence for safety and effectiveness of landiolol enrolled pediatric patients. While Kato et al. 1997 only excluded patients &lt;16 years old, from the reported demographics it appears that the youngest enrolled patient was 21 years old (Table 6). The sponsor has an ongoing clinical trial of landiolol for pediatric patients.
L1023: ## 8.8.4. **Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound**
L1024: Landiolol does not appear to have potential for abuse. It is expected that cessation of landiolol infusion with concomitant supportive care and vasopressor therapy initiation will be the most expeditious management options in the setting of overdose, but no known overdosing events were reported in any of the clinical trials published for landiolol treatment of SVT. In the clinical trials of landiolol there was no evidence of substantial tachycardic rebound following cessation of landiolol therapy.35
L1025: ## 8.9. **Safety in the Postmarket Setting**
L1026: ## 8.9.1. **Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience**
L1027: No new relevant safety issues have been identified in the most recent Periodic Safety Update Report (PSUR) from 03-Aug-2016 to 02-Feb-2022, as described in more detail in Section 8.9.2. Similar rates of hypotension, at or less than 10%, have been described in the post-marketing setting in this PSUR, which includes an estimate of [REDACTED] (b) (4) landiolol-treated patients.
L1028: ## 8.9.2. **Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting**
L1029: There is substantial post-market data from Japan and Europe for landiolol and no additional adverse safety signals have been identified. The post-marketing report submission covers the period from 03-Aug-2016 to 02-Feb-2022. The identified risks in those patients are severe
L1030: 35 Yoshiya I, Ogawa R, Okumura F, et al. Clinical evaluation of landiolol hydrochloride (ONO-1101) on peri-operative supraventricular tachyarrhythmia- a phase III, double-blind study in comparison with placebo. Rinsho Iyaku (Jpn. J. Clin. Therap. Med.) 1997;13(19):4949(129)-4978(158).
L1031: hypotension, severe bradycardia, and three instances of cardiac arrest. Three cases of cardiac arrest occurred in clinical studies; one in a patient with sick sinus syndrome and was a bradycardic arrest for reported 5-seconds. The outcome of the case was not reported. Two additional cases of cardiac arrest were reported in clinical studies, but with limited additional data, and both patients were reported to have recovered. During the reporting period from 03-Aug-2016 to 02-Feb-2022 three additional cases of cardiac arrest in the estimated (b) (4) patients were reported, one of which occurred 37 hours after completion of landiolol administration but was fatal, one of which occurred in a patient with left ventricular dysfunction who recovered afterwards, and one of which occurred in the setting of likely overdose at approximately 117 mcg/kg bolus compared to the labelled infusion rate of 1-(b) (4) mcg/kg/min.
L1032: The absence of the bolus infusion in the proposed label, contrasting with the approved indications in the European Union and Japan, is not expected to alter the safety of landiolol. Based on the information available, a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) does not appear to be necessary to ensure the benefits of landiolol outweigh the risks.
L1033: ## 8.9.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L1034: No additional safety issues were identified.
L1035: ## 8.10. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1036: Based on the sum of the evidence from multiple randomized controlled clinical trials, including 6 placebo-controlled trials and 2 active-controlled trials, landiolol appears to be safe in the context of cardiac monitoring for reducing HR for patients with SVT including AF and AFL. The principal safety event associated with landiolol administration in the randomized controlled treatment trials, as well as in patients treated for the prevention of SVT and those patients with reported AEs in the post-market setting, is hypotension. In the hospitalized and monitored setting, hypotension is monitorable, and based on the published data, reversible with landiolol discontinuation. Although the clinical trials were performed exclusively outside of the US, principally in Japan and China, the data are clinically applicable to patients in the US, and it is expected that patients in the US will respond similarly to this beta-adrenergic receptor antagonist as they do to other beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists that have supportive data in the US population.
L1037: - No individual patient safety data was submitted as part of the application. Safety assessment was only from the published captured adverse events, with the potential for missed ascertainment and no avenue to interrogate the primary medical records, ascertainment protocols, or investigators for the submitted clinical trials.
- The risk of hypotension varied by the population studied in the submitted published data, but no specific predictors of hypotension were identified. Although the inclusion criteria with regards to enrollment BP and HR were not consistently defined, hypotension was in all cases monitorable and reversible.
L1038: ## Landiolol hydrochloride
L1039: - Although the rate of reported adverse events from large numbers of submitted observational studies was lower, hypotension occurred in 44 of 445 patients included in the randomized placebo-controlled trials, giving a rate of 9.9%, compared to 5 of the 395 patients who received placebo, with a rate of 1.3%. The publications describe the hypotensive effects of landiolol to be monitorable and reversible over the course of 30 minutes with landiolol discontinuation.
- The dose of landiolol utilized for patients with known heart failure was lower than for those patients without heart failure, and the dose was titrated to a lower maximal dose and increased at a slower rate.
L1040: Based on the data submitted from 6 randomized placebo-controlled trials and 2 randomized active-controlled trials, landiolol caused a predictable, monitorable, and reversible decrease in blood pressure in the treated populations with associated clinical hypotension. Few patients required landiolol discontinuation due to hypotension, however in the case of discontinuation the hypotension was typically resolved within 30 minutes. The AE of hypotension is thus both clinically monitorable and reversible with landiolol removal. General regulatory understanding of the mechanism of action through beta-adrenergic receptor antagonism and the use of similar medications for this patient population add to the regulatory comfort with this application. Therefore, the applicant has provided substantial evidence of safety of landiolol for the intended population of patients with SVT including patients with AF and AFL, and no additional risk management strategies are required beyond the recommended labeling with expected continuous monitoring of HR and BP in the hospital setting, as well as use of lower dose with slower titration of landiolol for patients with heart failure.
L1041: ## 9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L1042: No advisory committee meeting occurred regarding this application, and no other external consultations were undertaken.
L1043: ## 10. Labeling Recommendations
L1044: ## 10.1. Prescription Drug Labeling
L1045: - The proposed label for landiolol is as “a beta-adrenergic blocker indicated for the short-term reduction of ventricular rate in patients with supraventricular tachycardia including atrial fibrillation and atrial flutter.”
- The labelled dosing is that for the free base rather than the landiolol hydrochloride to
L1046: maintain consistency in labeling across drug products.
L1047: (b) (4)
L1048: - and the patient monitored for effects on HR and BP.
- The proposed starting dose of 10 mcg/kg/min for patients without heart failure, titrated in 10 mcg/kg/min increments according to HR response, to a maximal dose of 40 mcg/kg/min is reasonable in the context of the dosing regimens tested in clinical trials.
- The proposed starting dose of 1 mcg/kg/min for patients with heart failure, titrated in 1 mcg/kg/min increments according to HR response, to a maximal dose of (b)/(4) mcg/kg/min is reasonable in the context of the dosing regimens tested in clinical trials.
- Maintenance infusions are expected to be continued for no longer than 24 hours and the weaning of landiolol and transition to alternative agents should consider the pharmacodynamics of the agent to which the patient is being transitioned.
- The contraindications, warnings, and precautions are those for beta-adrenergic receptor antagonists generally, and the most common adverse reaction was hypotension, occurring in approximately 9.9% of landiolol-treated patients in the published randomized placebo-controlled trial populations.
- Landiolol was demonstrated to be effective at lowering HR for patients with heart failure and left ventricular systolic dysfunction, with left ventricular ejection fraction between 25% and 50% but without systolic BP&lt;90 mmHg, when utilized at a lower dose with slower titration of dosing.
- Due to the risk of hypotension, bradycardia, and worsening cardiac failure, the safety recommendation is to use landiolol in a monitored setting and to monitor for signs and symptoms of cardiovascular adverse effects and reduce or discontinue use if these cardiovascular adverse effects occur.
- Due to the inadequate pharmacologic evaluation of landiolol in patients with hepatic dysfunction, landiolol use should be avoided in patients with Child-Pugh Class B or Class C hepatic dysfunction.
L1049: ## 10.2. Nonprescription Drug Labeling
L1050: Landiolol is a prescription drug.
L1051: ## 11. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L1052: The risks of landiolol, principally hypotension, can be adequately managed in the postmarket setting through product labeling alone. The hypotension that occurs with landiolol occurs in

approximately 10% of patients and is reversible with product removal. Given the rapid resolution of pharmacologic effect when drug administration is ceased, no additional safety measures are required apart from close monitoring of blood pressure when the drug is administered. A REMS does not appear to be necessary to ensure the benefits of landiolol outweigh the risks.

The graph shows the percentage change in HR over time for two groups: Group A (Landiolol, blue line) and Group P (Placebo, black line). The Y-axis represents the percentage change in HR, ranging from -50% to 10%. The X-axis represents time points, including PREPre-, AnestInit, Aneststable, and subsequent minutes (1 to 11 minutes during administration, and 5 to 60 minutes after discontinuation). Error bars represent mean \pm S.D.
The graph is divided into two main sections: the initial effect during administration (left side) and the persistence after discontinuation (right side).
Initial Effect (Minutes 1-11): Both groups show a rapid decrease in HR. Group A (Landiolol) achieves a significantly greater reduction in HR compared to Group P (Placebo). At 11 minutes, Group A shows approximately a 26.8% reduction, while Group P shows approximately a 11.5% reduction. The overall difference is statistically significant (p=0.0001).
Persistence (Minutes 5-60 after discontinuation): After discontinuation, Group A maintains a lower HR compared to Group P. At 30 minutes, Group A HR is approximately 91.6 bpm, and Group P HR is approximately 95.0 bpm. The difference is not statistically significant.

L1099: Figure 10 is a line graph showing Heart Rate (HR) in bpm over time (Pre, 0.5 h, 1 h, 2 h) for Landiolol (blue line) and Digoxin (red line). Landiolol shows a greater decrease in HR compared to Digoxin. Both drugs show a statistically significant decrease in HR over time (P < 0.0001).
L1100: Line graph showing Heart rate (beats/min) versus Time of administration (min) for Landiolol and Placebo groups. The Landiolol group shows a significant reduction in heart rate starting at 1 minute, reaching approximately 80 beats/min by 11 minutes. The Placebo group heart rate remains stable around 105 beats/min. Asterisks indicate statistical significance versus placebo: **** (P < .0001) and * (P < .05).
L1101: Figure 12 is a line graph illustrating the blood pressure response to landiolol (Group A) compared to placebo (Group P) over time. The Y-axis represents blood pressure in mmHg, ranging from 40 to 160. The X-axis represents time points: 5 min before pre-administration, pre-administration, 6 min, 11 min, 5 min, 10 min, and 15 min. A shaded bar indicates the "Drug infusion" period, spanning from pre-administration to 11 min.
Legend:
Group A (n = 35#,†): Systolic Blood Pressure (solid line with filled circles) and Diastolic Blood Pressure (solid line with open circles).
Group P (n = 40#): Systolic Blood Pressure (dashed line with filled triangles) and Diastolic Blood Pressure (dashed line with open triangles).
The graph shows that both systolic and diastolic blood pressure decrease slightly in both groups during the drug infusion period (pre-administration to 11 min). After the end of administration, blood pressure remains relatively stable, with Group A generally showing slightly lower values than Group P.
L1102: Line graph showing blood pressure response (mmHg) over time (minutes) for SBP (Systolic Blood Pressure) and DBP (Diastolic Blood Pressure) in two groups (Group A and Group P) after administration of landiolol. The Y-axis is mmHg (20 to 180). The X-axis shows time points: PRE, Pre-Anest, Anest, stable, 1 min, 2 min, 3 min, 4 min, 5 min, 6 min, 7 min, 8 min, 9 min, 10 min, 11 min, and then 5 min, 10 min, 15 min, 20 min, 30 min, 40 min, 50 min, 60 min (end). Data points are mean \pm S.D.
Legend: SBP Group A (solid line), SBP Group P (dashed line), DBP Group A (solid line), DBP Group P (dashed line).
Statistical significance is indicated: p=0.0001 for SBP and p=0.0002 for DBP.

L1106: Figure 17: Line graph showing Systolic blood pressure (mmHg) over Time of administration (min) for Landiolol and Placebo groups. Landiolol shows a significant reduction in SBP compared to Placebo, marked by asterisks (*), starting around 1 minute and continuing until approximately 5 minutes.
